=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
KEYENCE is famous for quick delivery.

In this problem, the calendar proceeds as Day 1, Day 2, Day 3, \dots.
There are orders 1,2,\dots,N, and it is known that order i will be placed on Day T_i.
For these orders, shipping is carried out according to the following rules.

- At most K orders can be shipped together.
- Order i can only be shipped on Day T_i or later.
- Once a shipment is made, the next shipment cannot be made until X days later.
- That is, if a shipment is made on Day a, the next shipment can be made on Day a+X.



For each day that passes from order placement to shipping, dissatisfaction accumulates by 1 per day.
That is, if order i is shipped on Day S_i, the dissatisfaction accumulated for that order is (S_i - T_i).
Find the minimum possible total dissatisfaction accumulated over all orders when you optimally schedule the shipping dates.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N K X
T_1 T_2 \dots T_N

Output

Print the answer as an integer.

Constraints


- All input values are integers.
- 1 \le K \le N \le 100
- 1 \le X \le 10^9
- 1 \le T_1 \le T_2 \le \dots \le T_N \le 10^{12}

Sample Input 1

5 2 3
1 5 6 10 12

Sample Output 1

2

For example, by scheduling shipments as follows, we can achieve a total dissatisfaction of 2, which is the minimum possible.

- Ship order 1 on Day 1.
- This results in dissatisfaction of (1-1) = 0, and the next shipment can be made on Day 4.


- Ship orders 2 and 3 on Day 6.
- This results in dissatisfaction of (6-5) + (6-6) = 1, and the next shipment can be made on Day 9.


- Ship order 4 on Day 10.
- This results in dissatisfaction of (10-10) = 0, and the next shipment can be made on Day 13.


- Ship order 5 on Day 13.
- This results in dissatisfaction of (13-12) = 1, and the next shipment can be made on Day 16.

Sample Input 2

1 1 1000000000
1000000000000

Sample Output 2

0

Sample Input 3

15 4 5
1 3 3 6 6 6 10 10 10 10 15 15 15 15 15

Sample Output 3

35


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's handle first batch separately for clarity:
dp = [dict() for _ in range(N+1)]
for c in range(1, min(K,N)+1):
    a = T[c-1]
    cost = c * a
    update dp[c][a] = min(cost)
Then for p from 1 to N-1:
  for d,cost in dp[p].items():
    maxc = min(K, N-p)
    for c in range(1,maxc+1):
       p2=p+c
       a = d + X
       if a < T[p2-1]: a = T[p2-1]
       newcost = cost + c*a
       update dp[p2][a]

Let's think about using arrays: For each p, we could have dict. Updating dp[p2][a] with min. We can use `if new < old` etc.

Implementation details:

```
import sys
def solve():
    input=sys.stdin.readline
    N,K,X=map(int,input().split())
    T=list(map(int,input().split()))
    total=sum(T)
    dp=[{} for _ in range(N+1)]
    # first batch
    for c in range(1, min(K,N)+1):
        a=T[c-1]
        cost=c*a
        d=dp[c]
        if a not in d or cost<d[a]:
            d[a]=cost
    for p in range(1,N+1):
        if not dp[p]:
            continue
        maxc=min(K,N-p)
        if maxc<=0: continue
        for d,cost in dp[p].items():
            base=d+X
            # maybe if base > T[-1]? no
            for c in range(1,maxc+1):
                p2=p+c
                a=base
                tp=T[p2-1]
                if a<tp:
                    a=tp
                nc=cost+c*a
                dd=dp[p2]
                old=dd.get(a)
                if old is None or nc<old:
                    dd[a]=nc
    ans=min(dp[N].values()) - total
    print(ans)
```

Need be careful `old is None` if cost can be None? Costs ints nonnegative, so use `if a not in dd or nc < dd[a]` maybe `dict.get(a, 10**30)`.

Now, let's think about if we should use `defaultdict(lambda: INF)`? Dict get okay.

Potential micro-optimizations:
- Precompute T list with dummy at index 0? Use 1-index for easier: `T = [0] + list(map(int,...))`. Then T[p2] works. SumT = sum(T) (including 0). This avoids `T[p2-1]`.
- In inner loop, `base = d + X`. For each c, `a = base if base >= T[p+c] else T[p+c]`.
- Use local variables for speed: `dp_local = dp`, `T_local = T`, `K_local = K`, `X_local = X`.
- Use `for c in range(1, maxc+1): p2 = p + c; tp = T[p2]; ...`
- Since N small, not critical.

Let's write code:

```
import sys

def solve():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N, K, X = map(int, input().split())
    T = [0] + list(map(int, input().split()))
    total = sum(T)
    dp = [dict() for _ in range(N+1)]
    lim = min(K, N)
    for c in range(1, lim+1):
        a = T[c]
        cost = c * a
        d = dp[c]
        if a not in d or cost < d[a]:
            d[a] = cost

    for p in range(1, N+1):
        if not dp[p]:
            continue
        maxc = min(K, N-p)
        if maxc <= 0:
            continue
        for d, cost in dp[p].items():
            base = d + X
            for c in range(1, maxc+1):
                p2 = p + c
                a = base
                tp = T[p2]
                if a < tp:
                    a = tp
                nc = cost + c * a
                dd = dp[p2]
                old = dd.get(a)
                if old is None or nc < old:
                    dd[a] = nc

    ans = min(dp[N].values()) - total
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```

Let's prove prefix transformation more rigorously in case of hidden issue: Given any feasible schedule, sort orders by shipping day? We want S_i nondecreasing in i. For any pair i<j with S_i > S_j, since T_i <= T_j <= S_j < S_i, order i is available at S_j. Swap their shipping days: assign i to S_j, j to S_i. Before final, let's revisit the proof of prefix transformation one more time because it's central. Suppose we have a feasible schedule with shipment days d_1<...<d_m and capacities c_l. Let S_i be the day order i is shipped. If there exist i<j with S_i > S_j, then T_i <= T_j <= S_j < S_i. Swap S_i and S_j. Need check order j can be shipped on S_i: S_i > S_j >= T_j, so yes. Order i can be shipped on S_j: S_j >= T_j >= T_i, so yes. The number of orders shipped on each day unchanged. Thus feasible and total sum S unchanged. Repeating bubble sort by S_i gives S_1<=S_2<=...<=S_N. Then for any day d_l, the set {i | S_i <= d_l} is a prefix. Since shipments are at distinct days, each shipment contains a contiguous block of indices. Thus there is an optimal schedule where batches are contiguous prefixes. Good.

Now, let's think about if the transformation to S_i nondecreasing might change the "order i" labels? We swap shipping days, so labels keep T_i. The final S_i nondecreasing. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "total sum S unchanged" after multiple swaps; yes each swap preserves sum.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "batches are contiguous" but if two shipments have same day? Not allowed X>=1, so no. If X=0, could have same day, but not constraints.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "DP assumes first p orders shipped by last day d" and if S_i nondecreasing, yes.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "cost = sum c_l a_l" and if batches are contiguous, the sum of shipping days over orders = sum over batches c_l * a_l. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "a_l = max(a_{l-1}+X, T_{C_l})" for a contiguous batch schedule. Need prove minimal shipment days for a given partition. Given previous shipment day a_{l-1}, the earliest possible day for batch l is a_{l-1}+X. Also all orders in batch l must be released by then; since batch contains orders C_{l-1}+1..C_l, the latest release is T_{C_l}. Thus a_l >= max(a_{l-1}+X, T_{C_l}). Choosing equality minimizes current and all future days. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "a_1 = T_{c_1}" and if previous day nonexistent. Could also set a_1 = max(0, T_{c_1}) but T>=1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "DP over p,d" and if we initialize first batch for all c, we are considering all possible first batch sizes. Good.

DP min won't choose if truly suboptimal. But let's prove suboptimal one more time with a potential counterexample where moving an available order to current shipment might delay a later shipment due to capacity? Wait, moving an available order to current shipment with spare capacity does not change shipment days at all. It simply changes which order is in which shipment. The later shipment loses an order, so its total number of orders decreases. All shipment days remain the same. The moved order's shipping day changes from later to current. There is no effect on any other order's shipping day. Therefore total sum S decreases by (later_day - current_day) >0. This is always feasible because current capacity was not full and order available. So any schedule with spare capacity and available unshipped order is not optimal. This is independent of prefix. Thus optimal schedules are work-conserving. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "prefix transformation" after moving might change total sum S? The moved schedule has lower total S. Then prefix transformation preserves total S. So there is an optimal prefix schedule with lower/equal total S. Good.
